========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess what brings me in is I’ve been noticing I keep making these little mistakes that feel like they shouldn’t be happening. Not huge disasters, but enough that it’s embarrassing and frustrating.

Like, at the senior center, I wrote down the wrong time on a class sheet — and I had looked at it, or I thought I had. Then later someone pointed it out, and I just thought, how did I miss that? And at home, I’ll be doing something simple, like measuring a board, and I’ll measure it once and still end up cutting it just a hair short because I skipped over some small print or didn’t catch a little detail I should’ve noticed.

It’s those kinds of things. I feel like I’m trying to pay attention, but these small errors keep slipping through anyway, and I’m not sure why.

Yeah, mostly with putting things down and then not being able to find them. I don’t know that I’d call it “memory” exactly, but it feels like I just had something in my hand, set it somewhere without thinking, and then five minutes later I’m wandering around looking for it.

Keys are a big one. My reading glasses too — I’ll take them off for a second, or I think I put them on the table, and then they’re not there, and I’m checking the same three places over and over. Same with my tape measure if I’m doing something around the condo. I’ll use it, put it down somewhere, then need it again and spend more time than I should hunting for it.

There’s also this little bag I keep card-game supplies in, and I’ve misplaced that a few times. It’s not like it’s gone forever, usually I find it, but it’s irritating because I know I had it just a few minutes before. So yes, in that sense, I have noticed it.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it’s completely new, now that you ask it that way. I guess I’ve always had some version of this, just maybe I didn’t think of it as a “problem” back then, or people around me sort of worked around it.

Even going way back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I’d forget my books or lose my homework. There were comments like that on report cards, if I’m remembering right. And I’d get caught staring out the window unless the teacher or somebody brought me back to what I was supposed to be doing.

So, yes, the condo move made me notice it more recently, but the pattern itself… I guess it’s not brand new. It’s been there in some form for a long time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do notice that sometimes. Not so much that I’m pacing all day or anything dramatic like that, but if I have to sit through a whole meeting, or even just a quiet evening at the condo, I can get this sort of ants-in-my-pants feeling.

It’s like I’ll be sitting there, and part of me is trying to stay with whatever’s going on, but another part is thinking, “I should get up and check that,” or “Maybe I’ll just go straighten something,” or “I’ll putter around for a minute.” Sometimes I’ll get up to look at something that probably could’ve waited. It’s not always urgent — it just feels hard to stay planted.

As far as speaking or acting without thinking, I’m less sure about that. The thing I notice more is the restlessness, wanting to get up and do something when I’m supposed to just sit still.

And at home, I’ll be doing something simple, like measuring a board, and I’ll measure it once and still end up cutting it just a hair short because I skipped over some small print or didn’t catch a little detail I should’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. I feel like I’m trying to pay attention, but these small errors keep slipping through anyway, and I’m not sure why.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I mess something up, and sometimes I’ll stew on it longer than I probably should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad all the time or like everything’s pointless.\n\nAs far as interest goes… I’m still doing the things I normally do. I might be annoyed or embarrassed when I make one of those mistakes, but it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do things I usually enjoy. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of low mood or loss of interest lately.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing these small mistakes, and would you say they've been happening about the same amount over time, or have they been getting more frequent?\n\nPatient: I’d say I really started noticing it after we moved into the condo — that was about a year and a month ago now, something like that. I mean, I’m sure I’ve made little mistakes before, everybody does, but since the move it’s been noticeable enough that it’s interfering, or at least getting in my way more than I’d like.\n\nAs far as whether it’s getting worse… I don’t know if I’d say it’s a sharp increase, like suddenly every day is worse than the last. It’s more that, over this past year or so, it’s been steady enough that I can’t just brush it off as a bad week. It keeps showing up in these small ways, and after a while you start thinking, okay, this isn’t just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides these small mistakes with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory, like forgetting recent conversations, appointments, or where you put things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with putting things down and then not being able to find them. I don’t know that I’d call it “memory” exactly, but it feels like I just had something in my hand, set it somewhere without thinking, and then five minutes later I’m wandering around looking for it.\n\nKeys are a big one. My reading glasses too — I’ll take them off for a second, or I think I put them on the table, and then they’re not there, and I’m checking the same three places over and over. Same with my tape measure if I’m doing something around the condo. I’ll use it, put it down somewhere, then need it again and spend more time than I should hunting for it.\n\nThere’s also this little bag I keep card-game supplies in, and I’ve misplaced that a few times. It’s not like it’s gone forever, usually I find it, but it’s irritating because I know I had it just a few minutes before. So yes, in that sense, I have noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were younger—say in your 20s, 30s, or 40s—did you notice similar issues with losing items or missing small details, or is this level of forgetfulness and mistake-making new for you over this past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s completely new, now that you ask it that way. I guess I’ve always had some version of this, just maybe I didn’t think of it as a “problem” back then, or people around me sort of worked around it.\n\nEven going way back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I’d forget my books or lose my homework. There were comments like that on report cards, if I’m remembering right. And I’d get caught staring out the window unless the teacher or somebody brought me back to what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo, yes, the condo move made me notice it more recently, but the pattern itself… I guess it’s not brand new. It’s been there in some form for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still, or do you often act or speak impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that sometimes. Not so much that I’m pacing all day or anything dramatic like that, but if I have to sit through a whole meeting, or even just a quiet evening at the condo, I can get this sort of ants-in-my-pants feeling.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be sitting there, and part of me is trying to stay with whatever’s going on, but another part is thinking, “I should get up and check that,” or “Maybe I’ll just go straighten something,” or “I’ll putter around for a minute.” Sometimes I’ll get up to look at something that probably could’ve waited. It’s not always urgent — it just feels hard to stay planted.\n\nAs far as speaking or acting without thinking, I’m less sure about that. The thing I notice more is the restlessness, wanting to get up and do something when I’m supposed to just sit still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, going way back, I’m not sure I would’ve called it “restless” the way people mean, like bouncing off the walls or getting in trouble for running around. I don’t think it was that obvious. But the feeling of not wanting to just sit there — that does feel familiar.

Like even now, if I’m in a meeting or if it’s just a quiet evening at the condo, I get that ants-in-my-pants feeling where I want to get up and check something, or go putter around, or straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening right then. And thinking back, I probably had some version of that when I was younger too — just needing to shift around or wanting to be doing something instead of sitting still.

I don’t really remember being a big blurter or acting wild without thinking, not in a clear way. It’s more that sitting still for too long has always felt uncomfortable, like I’m fighting the urge to get up and do some little thing.

And at home, I’ll be doing something simple, like measuring a board, and I’ll measure it once and still end up cutting it just a hair short because I skipped over some small print or didn’t catch a little detail I should’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. I feel like I’m trying to pay attention, but these small errors keep slipping through anyway, and I’m not sure why.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I mess something up, and sometimes I’ll stew on it longer than I probably should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad all the time or like everything’s pointless.\n\nAs far as interest goes… I’m still doing the things I normally do. I might be annoyed or embarrassed when I make one of those mistakes, but it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do things I usually enjoy. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of low mood or loss of interest lately.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing these small mistakes, and would you say they've been happening about the same amount over time, or have they been getting more frequent?\n\nPatient: I’d say I really started noticing it after we moved into the condo — that was about a year and a month ago now, something like that. I mean, I’m sure I’ve made little mistakes before, everybody does, but since the move it’s been noticeable enough that it’s interfering, or at least getting in my way more than I’d like.\n\nAs far as whether it’s getting worse… I don’t know if I’d say it’s a sharp increase, like suddenly every day is worse than the last. It’s more that, over this past year or so, it’s been steady enough that I can’t just brush it off as a bad week. It keeps showing up in these small ways, and after a while you start thinking, okay, this isn’t just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides these small mistakes with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory, like forgetting recent conversations, appointments, or where you put things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with putting things down and then not being able to find them. I don’t know that I’d call it “memory” exactly, but it feels like I just had something in my hand, set it somewhere without thinking, and then five minutes later I’m wandering around looking for it.\n\nKeys are a big one. My reading glasses too — I’ll take them off for a second, or I think I put them on the table, and then they’re not there, and I’m checking the same three places over and over. Same with my tape measure if I’m doing something around the condo. I’ll use it, put it down somewhere, then need it again and spend more time than I should hunting for it.\n\nThere’s also this little bag I keep card-game supplies in, and I’ve misplaced that a few times. It’s not like it’s gone forever, usually I find it, but it’s irritating because I know I had it just a few minutes before. So yes, in that sense, I have noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were younger—say in your 20s, 30s, or 40s—did you notice similar issues with losing items or missing small details, or is this level of forgetfulness and mistake-making new for you over this past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s completely new, now that you ask it that way. I guess I’ve always had some version of this, just maybe I didn’t think of it as a “problem” back then, or people around me sort of worked around it.\n\nEven going way back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I’d forget my books or lose my homework. There were comments like that on report cards, if I’m remembering right. And I’d get caught staring out the window unless the teacher or somebody brought me back to what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo, yes, the condo move made me notice it more recently, but the pattern itself… I guess it’s not brand new. It’s been there in some form for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still, or do you often act or speak impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that sometimes. Not so much that I’m pacing all day or anything dramatic like that, but if I have to sit through a whole meeting, or even just a quiet evening at the condo, I can get this sort of ants-in-my-pants feeling.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be sitting there, and part of me is trying to stay with whatever’s going on, but another part is thinking, “I should get up and check that,” or “Maybe I’ll just go straighten something,” or “I’ll putter around for a minute.” Sometimes I’ll get up to look at something that probably could’ve waited. It’s not always urgent — it just feels hard to stay planted.\n\nAs far as speaking or acting without thinking, I’m less sure about that. The thing I notice more is the restlessness, wanting to get up and do something when I’m supposed to just sit still.\n\nDoctor: Going back to your childhood or teenage years, did you notice yourself being unusually restless, fidgety, or impulsive—like blurting things out or acting before thinking—the way you described feeling recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, going way back, I’m not sure I would’ve called it “restless” the way people mean, like bouncing off the walls or getting in trouble for running around. I don’t think it was that obvious. But the feeling of not wanting to just sit there — that does feel familiar.\n\nLike even now, if I’m in a meeting or if it’s just a quiet evening at the condo, I get that ants-in-my-pants feeling where I want to get up and check something, or go putter around, or straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening right then. And thinking back, I probably had some version of that when I was younger too — just needing to shift around or wanting to be doing something instead of sitting still.\n\nI don’t really remember being a big blurter or acting wild without thinking, not in a clear way. It’s more that sitting still for too long has always felt uncomfortable, like I’m fighting the urge to get up and do some little thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is I’ve been noticing I keep making these little mistakes that feel like they shouldn’t be happening. Not huge disasters, but enough that it’s embarrassing and frustrating.\n\nLike, at the senior center, I wrote down the wrong time on a class sheet — and I had looked at it, or I thought I had. Then later someone pointed it out, and I just thought, how did I miss that? And at home, I’ll be doing something simple, like measuring a board, and I’ll measure it once and still end up cutting it just a hair short because I skipped over some small print or didn’t catch a little detail I should’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. I feel like I’m trying to pay attention, but these small errors keep slipping through anyway, and I’m not sure why.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I mess something up, and sometimes I’ll stew on it longer than I probably should, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad all the time or like everything’s pointless.\n\nAs far as interest goes… I’m still doing the things I normally do. I might be annoyed or embarrassed when I make one of those mistakes, but it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do things I usually enjoy. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that kind of low mood or loss of interest lately.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing these small mistakes, and would you say they've been happening about the same amount over time, or have they been getting more frequent?\n\nPatient: I’d say I really started noticing it after we moved into the condo — that was about a year and a month ago now, something like that. I mean, I’m sure I’ve made little mistakes before, everybody does, but since the move it’s been noticeable enough that it’s interfering, or at least getting in my way more than I’d like.\n\nAs far as whether it’s getting worse… I don’t know if I’d say it’s a sharp increase, like suddenly every day is worse than the last. It’s more that, over this past year or so, it’s been steady enough that I can’t just brush it off as a bad week. It keeps showing up in these small ways, and after a while you start thinking, okay, this isn’t just a one-time thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides these small mistakes with details, have you noticed any changes in your memory, like forgetting recent conversations, appointments, or where you put things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with putting things down and then not being able to find them. I don’t know that I’d call it “memory” exactly, but it feels like I just had something in my hand, set it somewhere without thinking, and then five minutes later I’m wandering around looking for it.\n\nKeys are a big one. My reading glasses too — I’ll take them off for a second, or I think I put them on the table, and then they’re not there, and I’m checking the same three places over and over. Same with my tape measure if I’m doing something around the condo. I’ll use it, put it down somewhere, then need it again and spend more time than I should hunting for it.\n\nThere’s also this little bag I keep card-game supplies in, and I’ve misplaced that a few times. It’s not like it’s gone forever, usually I find it, but it’s irritating because I know I had it just a few minutes before. So yes, in that sense, I have noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were younger—say in your 20s, 30s, or 40s—did you notice similar issues with losing items or missing small details, or is this level of forgetfulness and mistake-making new for you over this past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s completely new, now that you ask it that way. I guess I’ve always had some version of this, just maybe I didn’t think of it as a “problem” back then, or people around me sort of worked around it.\n\nEven going way back to elementary school, I remember teachers saying things like I was bright, I understood the material, but I’d forget my books or lose my homework. There were comments like that on report cards, if I’m remembering right. And I’d get caught staring out the window unless the teacher or somebody brought me back to what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo, yes, the condo move made me notice it more recently, but the pattern itself… I guess it’s not brand new. It’s been there in some form for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still, or do you often act or speak impulsively without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice that sometimes. Not so much that I’m pacing all day or anything dramatic like that, but if I have to sit through a whole meeting, or even just a quiet evening at the condo, I can get this sort of ants-in-my-pants feeling.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be sitting there, and part of me is trying to stay with whatever’s going on, but another part is thinking, “I should get up and check that,” or “Maybe I’ll just go straighten something,” or “I’ll putter around for a minute.” Sometimes I’ll get up to look at something that probably could’ve waited. It’s not always urgent — it just feels hard to stay planted.\n\nAs far as speaking or acting without thinking, I’m less sure about that. The thing I notice more is the restlessness, wanting to get up and do something when I’m supposed to just sit still.\n\nDoctor: Going back to your childhood or teenage years, did you notice yourself being unusually restless, fidgety, or impulsive—like blurting things out or acting before thinking—the way you described feeling recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, going way back, I’m not sure I would’ve called it “restless” the way people mean, like bouncing off the walls or getting in trouble for running around. I don’t think it was that obvious. But the feeling of not wanting to just sit there — that does feel familiar.\n\nLike even now, if I’m in a meeting or if it’s just a quiet evening at the condo, I get that ants-in-my-pants feeling where I want to get up and check something, or go putter around, or straighten something that probably doesn’t need straightening right then. And thinking back, I probably had some version of that when I was younger too — just needing to shift around or wanting to be doing something instead of sitting still.\n\nI don’t really remember being a big blurter or acting wild without thinking, not in a clear way. It’s more that sitting still for too long has always felt uncomfortable, like I’m fighting the urge to get up and do some little thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently interrupting others in conversation, finishing their sentences, or having trouble waiting your turn when talking or standing in line?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me, at least not in a way I’ve really noticed. I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly cutting people off or finishing their sentences. I mean, I probably have done it here and there, like anybody does, especially if I think I know where someone is going with something, but I wouldn’t say people complain about that to me.\n\nWaiting in line… I don’t love it, but I don’t think I do anything especially impulsive. I might get impatient internally, or sort of look around and think about how long it’s taking, but I don’t usually step out of turn or say something. So… no, I guess I wouldn’t put that near the top of the list. The sitting still feeling is more noticeable to me than interrupting people or not waiting my turn.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

The sitting still feeling is more noticeable to me than interrupting people or not waiting my turn.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say these issues with losing things, missing details, and restlessness cause noticeable problems in more than one area of your life—for example, both at home and when you're out at the senior center or with friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"The mix-ups have made me step back from helping lead a couple of DIY workshops, and my wife has had to double-check plans so I do not throw off family visits or center activities.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I would say it shows up in more than one place, not just at home. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart, but it does create these little problems that add up.

At the senior center, for example, I used to help more with the DIY workshops — not necessarily running everything by myself, but helping lead parts of them, keeping track of materials, steps, that kind of thing. And after a couple of mix-ups, I’ve kind of stepped back from that. I don’t like saying that, because I enjoyed feeling useful, but if I miss a detail or misplace something, it can throw off the whole activity, and then I feel embarrassed.

And at home, my wife has had to double-check plans more than she probably should. Like if we’re coordinating family visits or something connected to the center, she’ll go over it again to make sure I didn’t get the time wrong, forget a piece of it, or mix something up. She’s kind about it, mostly, but I can tell it puts more on her. So yes, it’s noticeable — mild maybe, but definitely noticeable in both places.

---------- turn 1 | judge:symptom_extraction | symptom_diagnosis.identify_symptoms_turn() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

  {

    "role": "user",
    "content": "You are a clinical psychiatrist reviewing a single patient utterance.\n\n=== SYMPTOM CATALOGUE ===\n[S001] Detail_Inattention_Errors: Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes.\n[S002] Sustained_Attention_Difficulty: Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\n[S003] Listening_Difficulty: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n[S004] Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n[S005] Organization_Difficulty: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n[S006] Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n[S007] Losing_Items: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n[S008] Distractibility: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n[S009] Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n[S010] Fidgeting: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n[S011] Leaving_Seat: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n[S012] Feeling_Restless: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Not huge disasters, but enough that it’s embarrassing and frustrating.\n\nLike, at the senior center, I wrote down the wrong time on a class sheet — and I had looked at it, or I thought I had. Then later someone pointed it out, and I just thought, how did I miss that?   }

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so, not in any big way.   }

Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, mostly with putting things down and then not being able to find them. I don’t know that I’d call it “memory” exactly, but it feels like I just had something in my hand, set it somewhere without thinking, and then five minutes later I’m wandering around looking for it.\n\nKeys are a big one. My reading glasses too — I’ll take them off for a second, or I think I put them on the table, and then they’re not there, and I’m checking the same three places over and over. Same with my tape measure if I’m doing something around the condo. I’ll use it, put it down somewhere, then need it again and spend more time than I should hunting for it.\n\nThere’s also this little bag I keep card-game supplies in, and I’ve misplaced that a few times. It’s not like it’s gone forever, usually I find it, but it’s irritating because I know I had it just a few minutes before. So yes, in that sense, I have noticed it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I’d say that’s a big issue for me, at least not in a way I’ve really noticed. I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly cutting people off or finishing their sentences. I mean, I probably have done it here and there, like anybody does, especially if I think I know where someone is going with something, but I wouldn’t say people complain about that to me.\n\nWaiting in line… I don’t love it, but I don’t think I do anything especially impulsive. I might get impatient internally, or sort of look around and think about how long it’s taking, but I don’t usually step out of turn or say something. The sitting still feeling is more noticeable to me than interrupting people or not waiting my turn.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
